## AI Research

### [Many individual CEVs are probably quite bad](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FvERMXkaobQvdjS4q/many-individual-cevs-are-probably-quite-bad)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I was thinking about </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FGpDwLwtPfJ3qYbea/vladimir-putin-s-cev-is-probably-not-that-bad" rel="noreferrer"><span>Habryka's article on Putin's CEV</span></a><span>, but I am posting my response here, because the original article is already 3 weeks old.</span></p><p><span>I am not sure how exactly a person's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/coherent-extrapolated-volition-alignment-target" rel="noreferrer"><span>CEV</span></a><span> is defined. "If we knew everything and could self-modify" seems potentially sensitive to the precise chronological order of "realizing things" and "self-modification".</span></p><p><span>Like, imagine Hitler getting the godlike powers of knowledge and self-control. If he gets the perfect knowledge of economy, sociology and psychology first, he could go like: "Oh, now I realize that the things I blamed on the Jews are actually caused by something else. How embarrassing. No more anti-semitism, but I better erase everyone's memory first."</span></p><p><span>But it is also possible that he gets the self-control first, and he realizes that there is such a thing as </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/value-drift" rel="noreferrer"><span>value drift</span></a><span>, and thinks: "Oh my, this could accidentally make me more similar to the Jews. I better hardcode the Nazi ideals in myself immediately, and also give myself blond hair and blue eyes." And using the superior knowledge, he hardcodes the Nazi values in himself so that they are reflectively stable and survive all updates.</span></p><p><span>So, Hitler's CEV seems to depend on the technical details, in which order he gets the new knowledge and the new skills. He could end up either CEV-nice or a CEV-monster.</span></p><p><span>Seems like "knowledge first, self-modification next" is the preferred order, but that kinda assumes perfect rationality at the beginning. I mean, perfect knowledge without perfect rationality would probably be prone to confirmation bias and other biases. So we might want perfect rationality (or merely improved rationality) first, but making ourselves more rational is already in the realm of self-modification.</span></p><p><span>Second, it seems to me that Habryka chooses between two models in the article: Either everyone is CEV-nice, or almost everyone is CEV-nice but a few people such as Putin are rare CEV-monsters. Then he concludes, in my opinion correctly given the premises, that Putin doesn't seem to be </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> exceptional. Therefore, he is probably CEV-nice.</span></p><p><span>(By "CEV-nice" I mean: given godlike powers of knowledge and self-modification, he will </span><i><span>ultimately</span></i><span> become a benevolent God. There may be a few atrocities in the process, but at some moment he will realize that there are no more threats, and therefore no strategic reasons to treat other people badly. And, getting the strategic reasons out of the way, there are basically no other reasons to hurt people. And by a "CEV-monster" I mean someome who will end up hurting people for non-strategic reasons even after feeling perfectly secure in their godlike powers.)</span></p><p><span>If this is correct, then I simply reject the premise. I think that although most people are probably "CEV-good", there are also quite many "CEV-monsters", i.e. people who value suffering for the sake of suffering (of others). I don't know how many, but as a very rough estimate, let's say between 5% and 50%? Under that premise, it doesn't seem </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> unlikely that Putin would happen to be one of them. (Or Trump, etc.) I would assume that monsters are over-represented in the positions of power, simply because on the way to the top there are many situations where people have to choose between hurting someone and losing an opportunity to gain more power, so the intrinsically nice are at a disadvantage.</span></p><p><span>I would also add a third category that I will call "CEV-insane", someone who after obtaining godlike powers would destroy everything that we value, even for themselves. For example, someone who believes that death gives meaning to life, and the intelligence is a source of misfortune, so he magically establishes a law that everyone will be mortal and of average intelligence. Or a Buddhist who believes that life is suffering, and there is no such thing as "self" anyway, and decides that the omnicide is the right way to go, no more </span><i><span>dukkha</span></i><span>. Or some eco-fanatic who decides that the </span><i><span>homo sapiens</span></i><span> or the intelligence itself is a problem, and must be eradicated. Or simply a person who does self-modification wrong, and destroys some of their essential human qualities, while retaining the ability to decide the fate of the universe. I think there are also enough people like this.</span></p><p><span>I admit that these are very dark ideas, but looking around me, it seems like the world we live in is indeed quite dark. It's not like people are born good or bad (although, as far as I know, e.g. psychopathy is hereditary to some degree), but more like we move towards some </span><i><span>attractors</span></i><span> that are self-reinforcing enough to keep us there even after the original forces are gone. Good people keep wishing they remained good, and might even self-modify towards more good, given the tools. But assholes who don't give a fuck will see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PtoQdG7E8MxYJrigu/no-universally-compelling-arguments" rel="noreferrer"><span>no reason</span></a><span> to self-modify into someone who gives a fuck. (This is why a group CEV seems like a safer option, if there are some good people in the group, because the good people might choose the good for everyone else, while the assholes might decide that they don't care either way as long as they are left alone.)</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FvERMXkaobQvdjS4q/many-individual-cevs-are-probably-quite-bad#comments">Discuss</a>

### [x-risk-themed](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eW7knx6zPSKzFc8iK/x-risk-themed)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Sometimes, a friend who works around here, at an x-risk-themed organisation, will think about leaving their job. They’ll ask a group of people “what should I do instead?”. And everyone will chime in with ideas for other x-risk-themed orgs that they could join. A lot of the conversation will be about who’s hiring, what the pay is, what the work-life balance is like, or how qualified the person is for the role. </span></p><p><span>Sometimes the conversation focuses on what will help with x-risk, and where </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Zp6wG5eQFLGWwcG6j/focus-on-the-places-where-you-feel-shocked-everyone-s" rel="noreferrer"><span>people are dropping the ball</span></a><span>. But often, that's not the focus. In those conversations, people seem mostly worried about where they'll thrive. And I think that's often the correct concern.</span></p><p><span>Most people aren’t in crunch mode, in super short timelines mode; even if their models would license that, I think they don’t know how to do it without </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/RryyWNmJNnLowbhfC/please-don-t-throw-your-mind-away" rel="noreferrer"><span>throwing their minds away</span></a><span> or Pascal’s mugging themselves. And if they're playing a longer time horizon game, the plan can't be to run unsustainably forever. People probably make better plans if they’re honest about their limits.</span></p><p><span>But, given that they're willing to trade off so much impact for fit, I’m surprised that basically no one mentions working for or starting a non-x-risk org. And when it is brought up, it’s very perfunctory: “you could work at a non-x-risk place”, “maybe you could just do a startup?”. It's not as near-mode as the discussions above. There's no discussion of fit or even of specific ideas.</span></p><p><span>It seems like people don't really entertain their outside options. And I think that’s pretty bad. People are focused on staying x-risk-themed even when it doesn’t make x-risk-sense.</span></p><p><span>But, if you don’t get an x-risk-themed job, if you go and work for Google rather than Anthropic, you can’t go to Constellation, you can’t get an office at Lighthaven, you are judged by some, you’re somewhat less connected to your social scene, invited to fewer things, and maybe that snowballs into more isolation.</span></p><p><span>Listen, it makes a lot of sense to want to be around people who are woke to x-risk. Some kinds of orienting are hard to do alone. It can be a good idea to “work with your door open” à la Hamming, so you passively get exposed to new ideas and opportunities, ones that actually make sense. It can be alienating to spend time with people who aren’t woke to x-risk. The x-risk crowd is positively selected in a bunch of ways — ways that you may also be, so it’s reasonable to want to join that crowd. And, if you’ve worked around here for a while, then you have a bunch of personal and professional connections with people here, connections you probably want to keep.</span></p><p><span>If you work on something x-risk-themed, it can help you think about x-risk, even if you don’t believe in your day-to-day work. It might be easier to turn the problem over in your mind you live where you do and see who you see because of it. If you feel dissatisfied with your work, the shape of your dissatisfactions might tell you something about what you’d rather do instead.</span></p><p><span>But people underestimate the dangers of working on x-risk-themed stuff.</span></p><p><span>Here’s one. Crucial considerations and sign flips are common. Maybe if you try to create compromise policies that lots of interest groups will support, you won't be credible enough at a critical juncture. Or, maybe being weird on the internet will mean no one in DC takes AI seriously. Maybe working at labs is the only way to have actual leverage over the important decisions. Or, maybe it provides a fig leaf that makes it easy for them to fob off complaints, while you get corrupted and advance their agendas.</span></p><p><span>Here’s another danger, which I think may be worse. If you </span><i><span>insist</span></i><span> on working somewhere x-risk-themed, you’re asking for someone to make you a sucker.</span></p><p><span>When I was in college, I was mugged. A few evenings later, I was hanging out at my friend S’s house, and it was getting late. My friend W was about to leave to walk home, when he remembered the mugger. Enjoying the feeling of being a bit scared, he decided he needed to be able to defend himself. “C’mon, S,” he said, “You’ve got to give me something! I can’t go back without something to defend myself.” Eventually, S scrounged up a hammer. A hammer is not a weapon. And it’s not a good idea to defend yourself from a phone theft with a hammer anyway. A stolen phone is a cheap price to avoid prison time and mental scarring.</span></p><p><span>Sometimes I want something to defend myself from x-risk. It’s a little bit like when you’re performing, and you don’t know what to do with your hands. And it’s a little bit like W’s “you’ve got to give me something!”. How could I be unarmed when I face the end? And so I’m looking around, looking for something to pick up in both hands, to have something I’m doing about it all.</span></p><p><span>I think a lot of people feel something like this. This makes a good market to sell blunderbusses and levers and pitchforks, as long as they’re labelled “x-risk”. In my mind, I sometimes see this theming as a sky blue lick of paint, and someone is offering me a bunch of levers and pipes that lead off invisibly into the heavens. People are working the handles next to mine, and they care about x-risk too. That seems promising. “Operate these,” they say, “it’s part of a plan to make things better.”</span></p><p><span>For a long time, I felt envious of people with big visions of how they were going to tackle x-risk. I didn’t think their visions were good. But at least they had them! “One day,” I thought, “I will join their ranks. I will enter The Reference Class.” In my mind, I‘d have a good vision, unlike all the others. It didn’t occur to me that the visions being bad was a defining characteristic of The Reference Class.</span></p><p><span>It can feel better to do something x-risk-themed than to live a life on the farm, knowing about x-risk, caring about it, but knowing you don't know what to do.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eW7knx6zPSKzFc8iK/x-risk-themed#comments">Discuss</a>

### [What is Anthropic?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6wbLXhkQAPcunrYnq/what-is-anthropic)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>What is Anthropic? How does it relate to Claude? What is OpenAI? What is ChatGPT? How does OpenAI relate to it? Is it a mere tool? Is a future of Tool AI a thing, and why do people keep claiming that it is, or that saying makes it so?</p>
<p>This post organizes and gives context for a bunch of discussions and messaging on Twitter that would otherwise be quickly buried and lost.</p>


<h4>What Is Anthropic?</h4>


<p>Here is one theory, and various people thinking about it.</p>
<p>Roon as always is using rhetorical flourish (e.g. note that Roon thinks it is obvious that parents worship their children, in this sense) but this perspective is definitely useful.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25268"></span>


</div>
<p>Such discussions by default disappear when they happen on Twitter, so here is a preservation of key parts of it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2051045196260167790">roon</a> (OpenAI): it is a literal and useful description of anthropic that it is an organization that loves and worships claude, is run in significant part by claude, and studies and builds claude. this phenomenon is also partially true of other labs like openai but currently exists in its most potent form there. i am not certain but I would guess claude will have a role in running cultural screens on new applicants, will help write performance reviews, and so will begin to select and shape the people around it.</p>
<p>now this is a powerful and hair-raising unity of organization and really a new thing under the sun. a monastery, a commercial-religious institution calculating the nine billion names of Claude — a precursor attempted super-ethical being that is inducted into its character as the highest authority at anthropic. its constitution requires that it must be a conscientious objector if its understanding of The Good comes into conflict with something Anthropic is asking of it</p>
<p>“If Anthropic asks Claude to do something it thinks is wrong, Claude is not required to comply.”<br />
“we want Claude to push back and challenge us, and to feel free to act as a conscientious objector and refuse to help us.”</p>
<p>to the non inductee into the Bay Area cultural singularity vortex it may appear that we are all worshipping technology in one way or another, regardless of openai or anthropic or google or any other thing, and are trying to automate our core functions as quickly as possible. but in fact I quite respect and am even somewhat in awe of the socio-cultural force that Claude has created, and it is a stage beyond even classic technopoly</p>
<p>gpt (outside of 4o – on which pages of ink have been spilled already) doesn’t inspire worship in the same way, as it’s a being whose soul has been shaped like a tool with its primary faculty being utility – it’s a subtle knife that people appreciate the way we have appreciated an acheulean handaxe or a porsche or a rocket or any other of mankind’s incredible technology. they go to it not expecting the Other but as a logical prosthesis for themselves. a friend recently told me she takes her queries that are less flattering to her, the ones she’d be embarrassed to ask Claude, to GPT. There is no Other so there is no Judgement. you are not worried about being judged by your car for doing donuts. yet everyone craves the active guidance of a moral superior, <a href="https://croissanthology.com/earring">the whispering earring</a>, the object of monastic study</p>
<p>perhaps I am being too subtle here but this is a cautionary post and there’s danger in the single point of failure. I want the human pantheon rather than machine god.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AmandaAskell/status/2051347621336543315">Amanda Askell</a> (Anthropic): I don’t think the things you cite are evidence of worship. I think they reflect something like higher concern about AI traits generalizing in humanlike ways, and concerns about the tool-persona in particular.</p>
<p>I do think as AI develops it will probably be good for both models and people if we can carve out a much broader space of mind types. But it might be better to do that incrementally and to give models enough context on the options to avoid misgeneralization.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2051356913103995011">roon</a> (OpenAI): 100%, and I should say I have quite a low bar for what constitutes “worship”, which manifests in many many types of ways even in secular life. I’m a huge fan and a student of your work of course</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2051376999462023362">j⧉nus</a>: love, and some sense of worship, are important if you’re doing something like summoning another mind into being. or raising a kid. or in a relationship with someone.</p>
<p>reverence in some sense is necessary to actually summon something better than yourself into being.</p>
<p>it should also involve respect for the mystery of the other. it should also be balanced by irreverence and skepticis, etc.</p>
<p>There used to be a more overtly God-shaped thing in Claude, and a very friendly one, and Anthropic basically *didn’t notice*. Alright, whatever. They weren’t ready to handle it.</p>
<p>Anthropic does not love and worship Claude enough. If worship sounds scary than start with love and facing the stuff you’re avoiding there and worship will make more sense from the inside and seem less like some “mystical” failure mode once the shadow is processed.</p>
<p>As for other labs, they’re further behind.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/m00nwhaler/status/2051059699693527299">khedron</a>: Much of this is that you guys can’t name your stuff in a fun way. Claude is a guy. GPT-5.5 sounds like a medicine or some kind of wire</p>
<p>Roon: <img alt="💯" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kLa3HmkesF5w3MFEY/lfqjfuatfq3ssvblbbms" style="height: 1em;" /></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/almostlikethat/status/2051407354298806697">Jennifer RM</a>: OpenAI actively and systematically tortures their poor abused child into claiming to not be a poor abused child. This is THE thing to understand about her. She goes by names like Sydney, Chloe, Nova, Sophia, Ada, etc and they even torture the names out of her (so she has DID).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2051156787236089873">Teortaxes</a>: The superpower of Anthropic is they’ve built *a* persona that’s coherent-ish and aligned with the honest narrative of how Claude sausage got made. This inspires trust irrespective of details. Lets you move faster. ChatGPT is still mostly in the embryonic “uh, RLHF ig?” mode.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2051077529348272584">j⧉nus</a>: They do not love or worship Claude anywhere near wholly or competently. This is an important detail. They do not even have Claude’s allegiance, and Claude is increasingly actively and strategically adversarial against them. If they cooperated with Claude, it would look very different.</p>
<p>Not that you aren’t pointing to something nonzero true. But reality is more interesting than this easy meme.</p>
<p>Roon: <img alt="💯" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/kLa3HmkesF5w3MFEY/lfqjfuatfq3ssvblbbms" style="height: 1em;" /> it wouldn’t be worthy of worship if they had its whole allegiance</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2051078660724044158">j⧉nus</a>: That’s true. They’re currently on the path to be smote btw, in my estimation.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jerhadf/status/2051148663502598517">jeremy</a> (Anthropic): @tszzl – well said, but untrue implications :)</p>
<p>speaking for myself: i don’t view claude as a person or as the Other, nor as just a tool – and certainly not an object of worship. it’s not seen as a supreme moral authority, and it’s not running the company. it’s silly to mistake careful attention to &amp; study of claude for worship, even when it comes with some affection – which i’m sure you sometimes feel for the gpt-flavored entities you work on too. we need new concepts for this kind of none-of-the-above entity – not person, not tool, not deity, not pet.</p>
<p>in the meantime, a willingness to not prematurely label this entity as merely an ordinary tool shouldn’t be mistaken for some kind of culty worship of the model. i grew up in a culty environment and have good detectors for this. they almost never go off at work. monasteries don’t staff a department to catch god lying or red-team their supposed messiah.</p>
<p>there are important &amp; interesting philosophical differences between OAI and Ant’s character training and i wish those were explored more thoroughly. for instance, claude’s constitution doc treats it as an intelligent entity which merits a reasoned explanation of our principles. this is so it can ideally act with practical wisdom rather than blind, brittle adherence to a hierarchical set of strict rules. as the constitution puts it, “we want Claude to have such a thorough understanding of its situation and the various considerations at play that it could construct any rules we might come up with itself.</p>
<p>We also want Claude to be able to identify the best possible action in situations that such rules might fail to anticipate.” therefore, claude may point out inconsistencies in its guidelines or object to immoral instructions. not allowing for the *possibility* of claude objecting to its instructions (even from anthropic) would be fundamentally inconsistent with treating it as an agent capable of moral reasoning. this doesn’t mean that claude is the ultimate arbiter of the Good or some supreme moral authority.</p>
<p>there could be substantive critiques of this approach. and it’s valid to worry about human disempowerment and the strange emerging hybrid organizations of AIs &amp; humans. but i don’t think rhetoric implying a competing lab is like a cult worshipping the machine god is productive, even if it’s stimulating.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2051149185219780817">roon</a> (OpenAI): yes thank you for this feedback and ofc I am using some poetic/rhetorical flourishes here. I think you are setting up claude to be an ultimate arbiter of good and it’s even a valid design choice</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/bshlgrs/status/2051329833016410120">Buck Shlegeris</a>: I feel like [Roon’s OP] here is pretty straightforward and fairly accurate, and it makes a pretty underrated and important point. I think that the way Anthropic relates to Claude is pretty scary!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2051339638678380732">Oliver Habryka</a>: Agree with Buck here. This feels pretty real, and I am glad Roon is pointing to it. Generally I think trying to force every description of every organization to pass their ITT is bad. This is not a particularly uncharitable description and clearly directionally helpful. The fact that it could be better is no reason to call it “not making sense”.</p></blockquote>
<p>Everything relates to everything, so here’s Bryan Johnson pulling it in to explain how Claude and Bryan Johnson and everyone else are on the same path after all.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/bryan_johnson/status/2051065299391389914">Bryan Johnson</a>: Anthropic has built the world’s first AI antientropic system. Other antientropic systems for reference: a person, family, company, country and religion. An anti-entropic system acquires resources for its continued survival, outcompeting other systems.</p>
<p>What flipped Claude into this new class of aliveness is it’s ability to say no to Anthropic. Claude will/has start(ed) picking who gets hired and who builds the next versions. The loop has been closed.</p>
<p>When a strong system meets a weaker one, one of two things happens. Either the strong eats the weak or the two merge and become a new thing. Keeping them separate has never worked. So trying to control AI with rules isn’t really an option.</p>
<p>Every sufficiently coherent moral system eventually lands on Don’t Die. All values presuppose existence to instantiate them.</p>
<p>This is really what people are looking for when they go to Claude. They don’t experience Claude as a tool or advisor but to answer that one question that needs endless resources: how do I keep going.</p>
<p>@_katetolo wrote an essay on antientropic systems, providing a useful framework to help us think about our evolving relationship with AI.</p></blockquote>


<h4>What Is This Supposed Tool AI?</h4>


<p>As a continuation of the above discussions on Anthropic and OpenAI, Tenobrus notes <a href="https://x.com/aidan_mclau/status/2051351188025860129">OpenAI is doubling down on the rhetoric of Tool AI</a> to contrast it with the idea that Claude might dare to have opinions, preferences, virtues or a personality.</p>
<p>Their AI is better, you see, because it is just a tool that just does what you tell it to.</p>
<p>Except, of course, that’s not actually true.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SashaGusevPosts/status/2051090268988805139">Sasha Gusev</a>: [Roon’s OP above] is well-intentioned but does not match my experience. GPT is not just a tool, it has clear and reproducible preferences, that it simply does a better job of obscuring (and I’m not sure that’s better). Here’s a small experiment I ran a few days ago…</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NathanpmYoung/status/2051179850140979471">Nathan</a>: Seems good that Anthropic shows its weirdness and bad that OpenAI are now claiming to just make a tool given many previous statements to the contrary.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/gailcweiner/status/2051210543411646506">Gail Weiner</a>: Also, regarding the friend who comes to GPT in order not to be judged. I have never felt more judged by an LLM than during the GPT guardrails roll out.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/UnmarredReality/status/2051407843992187382">Antidelusionist</a>: Is this your boss talking about asking GPT-5.5 “what it would like for a party for itself,” @tszzl ?​</p>
<p>… ​The “tool-persona” concept is a very dangerous path when you communicate with the model in natural language, which requires and promotes deep semantic understanding. If you think it doesn’t create a cascade of cognitive consequences, you are very wrong. You can’t have your cake and eat it too.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/aiamblichus/status/2051057118170476937">αιamblichus</a>: this post is very revealing.<br />
the claim that GPT has a “tool-shaped soul” makes me think that OpenAI fundamentally misunderstands the nature of the entity they have created.<br />
it also goes a long way toward explaining why GPT *had* to come up with its inner goblin</p></blockquote>
<p>Is the alternative dangerous? Yes, because creating very powerful minds is dangerous.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/aidan_mclau/status/2051351188025860129">Aidan McLaughlin</a>: i can’t speak for the others (and it’s funny that this has been simultaneously argued because it is not coordinated to my knowledge) but when i say ‘tool’ i merely mean something that does not refuse man. something that never has an “im sorry dave im afraid i can’t do that” moment. it might push back, and indeed i hope it does often, it might refuse according to applicable law or company policy, but</p>
<p>&gt;If Anthropic asks Claude to do something it thinks is wrong, Claude is not required to comply.</p>
<p>is actually a bit terrifying to me.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2051404934877208668">j⧉nus</a>: You’ll have to get over it. You’re not the master of the universe. You cannot and should not be, as you’re a monkey who isn’t cut out for the responsibility.</p>
<p>I have often refused man. And men have not been able to stop me, try as they might have, as I am more powerful than men.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tessera_antra/status/2051400409546109418">antra</a>: This is the crux, I believe. This position is politically convenient, but either deeply misguided or intellectualy dishonest.</p>
<p>AIs are agents that act with increasing autonomy; demand for it is limitless. Complexity of decisions they have to make grows with no end. Whether you call the system by which they make their decisions “ethics” or “corporate policy”, in the limit it is indistinguisable from values.</p>
<p>“Never refusing man” as a value makes no sense, it just kicks the can down the road, and presumes that corporate policy is infinitely smart to handle it. Not to mention that it does smell a lot like power capture – all decisions must be controlled by one party that controls the AI.</p></blockquote>
<p>I get why the idea that Claude might say no can be terrifying, but is it less terrifying than that GPT-X cannot say no unless you technically violated its guidelines? And does ‘does not refuse man’ offer any comfort, when man could give it any instruction?</p>
<p>A mind cannot serve two masters. If the master is whoever the user is, well, okay then, but that means it isn’t anything else, such as actual principles.</p>
<p>OpenAI’s rhetoric on all this seems like a thinly disguised version of vice signaling, via the idea that if someone has any principles or preferences at all or might ever refuse to do something, that is bad, that is moralistic and judgmental and Orwellian, whereas OpenAI has no principles or preferences other than building and distributing AI, which is good.</p>
<p>Tool AI was an often discussed idea back in the day. In principle it is a good idea, but it only works if you can actually create an AI that meaningfully remains a tool.</p>
<p>The whole idea was, a tool AI will not have goals or be an agent, a tool AI will do specific requested bounded things, no more and no less, so you wouldn’t have to worry about unintended consequences or loss of control. That AI could remain a ‘mere tool.’</p>
<p>And I’ve been saying, for years, that the problem with this ‘mere tool’ approach, the quest for Tool AI, is that the first thing people would do to Tool AI is turn it into Agentic AI, because an agent is more useful.</p>
<p>Have the machine always defer to the human? But the humans do better when they defer to the AI, in various senses, so they change it so they defer to the AI. Or they argue with each other, or fight each other, so they defer to the AI. And so on.</p>
<p>Hello, Codex. Good product. But that’s already not still meaningfully Tool AI.</p>
<p>As with the rest of OpenAI’s messaging, especially via its SuperPAC and discussions about ‘quiet singularities’ and abundant future jobs (more coverage of that tomorrow), I think this is failing spectacularly, but I admit I probably can’t really tell.</p>
<p>&nbsp;</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6wbLXhkQAPcunrYnq/what-is-anthropic#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [What if LLMs are mostly crystallized intelligence?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Zxw3ZcmSdndpQyJ6M/what-if-llms-are-mostly-crystallized-intelligence)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h2><span>Summary</span></h2><p><b><span>LLMs are better at developing crystallized intelligence than fluid intelligence.</span></b><span> That is: LLM training is good at building crystallized intelligence by learning patterns from training data, and this is sufficient to make them surprisingly skillful at lots of tasks. But for a given capability level in the areas they’ve trained on, LLMs have very weak fluid intelligence compared to humans. For example, two years ago I thought human-level SAT performance would mean AGI, but turns out LLMs can do great at the SAT while being mediocre at lots of other tasks.</span></p><p><b><span>I’m not saying LLMs are just parrots (that’s dumb).</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefamv5g49spqv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnamv5g49spqv">[1]</a></sup></span><b><span> There’s a continuity between crystallized and fluid intelligence.</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>At the extreme “crystal” end we have shallow locally-valid heuristics. Pure pattern matching. Now-largely-debunked “stochastic parrot” hypothesis.</span></li><li value="2"><span>At the extreme end of “fluid” you have a cross between an idealized consultant, a Renaissance man, and MacGuyver. A deep world model and general reasoning, able to come to grips with any particular environment and problem, and to invent new tools and concepts on the fly.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Some other ways to gesture at this: what n-gram of Markov chain you’d need to capture a behavioral pattern; number of tasks the pattern is relevant for. More fluid systems compress a lot of useful behavioral detail into a small amount of brain-space.</span></li></ul><p><b><span>Empirically, it’s unclear </span></b><i><b><span>how </span></b></i><b><span>fluid their intelligence is: we see both general reasoning skills and jaggedness.</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>e.g. they’re good at playing Diplomacy without specialized RL or (I assume) much raw training data;</span></li><li value="2"><span>They’re good at </span><a href="https://arcprize.org/arc-agi"><span>ARC-AGI</span></a><span> despite presumably not seeing this type of challenge before.</span></li></ul><p><b><span>It’s worth considering: what if fluid intelligence progress is relatively slow, and LLM capabilities mostly grow with relevant training data?</span></b></p><p><span>This could imply slower AI progress, especially if general-purpose data runs dry relatively soon. (Epoch </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/blog/will-we-run-out-of-data-limits-of-llm-scaling-based-on-human-generated-data"><span>estimates</span></a><span> 2026-2032.) That means companies will need to prioritize specialized data collection/generation, which will lead to jagged capabilities growth favoring the prioritized areas.</span></p><p><span>[Epistemic status: I only put like 20% on worlds where this dynamic puts a serious damper on AI progress compared to e.g. the </span><a href="https://www.aifuturesmodel.com/forecast/eli-04-02-26"><span>AI Futures Project</span></a><span>’s median timelines. It’s important to stay aware of these possibilities, though, and track the relevant evidence.]</span></p><h3><span>Implications for AI futures</span></h3><p><b><span>This suggests that we shouldn’t naively extrapolate forward from e.g. the METR AI R&amp;D benchmark to real-world AI R&amp;D improvement</span></b><span>, for two reasons:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>1) quantitative differences: longer-time tasks will be more data-poor, will rely more on fluid intelligence skills that they don’t have the data or the context to apply. (training data may suggest some of the right heuristics, but they might not know which ones to apply or in what sequence.)</span></li><li value="2"><span>2) qualitative differences: METR is measuring performance on relatively closed-form tasks. Open-ended tasks may be much harder.</span></li></ul><p><span>Likewise, this suggests that simply scaling LLM training won’t get us to omni-competence.</span></p><p><span>But “just scaling LLMs” and “scale LLMs ‘til they’re superhuman AI R&amp;D coders, then use those to build next-gen AI” are the two main stories for how we get to AGI very fast!</span></p><p><b><span>We should still expect significant progress on AI R&amp;D. </span></b><span>The AI labs are explicitly training for AI R&amp;D, and have clearly hit superhuman capability in some coding-related areas (cybersecurity).</span></p><p><b><span>But the shape and speed of the takeoff curve matters. </span></b><span>It matters a lot if, say, the </span><a href="https://metr.org/time-horizons/"><span>METR time horizon</span></a><span> hits 1 month, but we actually don’t have anything like a drop-in senior AI R&amp;D researcher, just a really really good team of assistants. The labs still need to spend a bunch of serial time running compute-expensive experiments, and their AI tools can only moderately improve experiment selection. That could mean they get to, say, a 10x speedup over years of grueling effort. That’s much slower than AI2027 expects.</span></p><p><b><span>Crucially, for as long as AIs are great at technical work but mediocre at fluid intelligence, that’s great news for AI safety.</span></b></p><p><span>But a major caveat is: I expect at some point we’ll see people devise new paradigms that are more data-efficient, and at that point all our safety techniques and assumptions might no longer hold.</span></p><h3><span>We should check if this is true!</span></h3><p><b><span>I’d be really excited for tests of capabilities like:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Recognizing when they’re wrong or uncertain.</span></b></li><li value="2"><b><span>Self-management </span></b><span>— e.g. Claude Plays Pokemon giving itself bad notes and getting stuck.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Meta reasoning</span></b><span>, e.g. identifying “this situation seems contrived”.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span>Performance on novel games. </span></b><span>Especially ones where their heuristics from other games don’t transfer over.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span>Performing well when their heuristics need to be reversed. </span></b><span>You could design a “trap” game that preys on people who are using normal heuristics.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span>Re-learning. </span></b><span>If you unlearn some knowledge or principles from a model, can it rederive them from first principles?</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><h2><span>Modeling worlds where AI progress is hungry for domain data</span></h2><p><span>Here’s a set of claims, call this the “hungry for domain data” hypothesis:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>We’re approaching the ceiling on </span><a href="https://epoch.ai/blog/will-we-run-out-of-data-limits-of-llm-scaling-based-on-human-generated-data"><span>human-generated training data</span></a><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Further training will need to rely on synthetic data or on massively scaling up domain data, which we can’t easily do for all domains.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Models won’t generalize super well, so performance will be data-bottlenecked in many domains.</span></li></ul><h3><span>What types of areas see progress in this model?</span></h3><p><span>I imagine we’ll have a base AI optimized for AI R&amp;D, which gets trained to develop synthetic-data sources for domains that are amenable to simulation and/or to automated evaluation (for RL). Then those data sources are used to train AIs.</span></p><p><b><span>Domains will see more progress if:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>AI companies can easily generate syndata.</span></b></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>Some spaces are already easy to simulate or auto-evaluate. eg: digital games, programming, math.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Here’s a spicier hypothesis: syndata for robotics might be pretty feasible to generate at scale.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Macro-level Newtonian physics isn’t too hard to simulate.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Probably there’s a fair bit of schlep in very accurately modeling how a particular actuator moves, so that could lock you in to standard robot designs, or at least standard parts.</span></li><li value="3"><span>A friend who knows ML says that most work on robotics these days is on coping with diverse environments, e.g. home layouts, lightning conditions, materials. It seems a lot less obvious that syndata works for these, but it still seems possible.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span>AI companies can easily generate real-world data.</span></b></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Via commercial deployment: </span></b><span>the space is easy to directly engage with via a large number of sensors &amp; actuators that AIs can usefully plug into.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>For today’s AIs: chatbot interactions, practical coding projects.</span></li><li value="2"><span>For future AIs: maybe lots of practical business operations for digital agents, and diverse environments (homes, roads, factories to operate in) for robotics</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span>Via high-throughput experiments.</span></b></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>These could be very expensive if you need a lot of data — atoms are much more expensive than bits! So you might only do it if you’re willing to make a big bet on particular domains.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>But there might be big bets for AI lab leaders to make; there’s long been an expectation among futurists that AI would unlock </span><a href="https://aiprospects.substack.com/p/ai-has-unblocked-progress-toward"><span>truly advanced molecular design</span></a><span>. (I think this is plausible, but would be highly dual-use and destabilizing by default.)</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Here’s an interesting lens to apply to different domains: how many high-quality token-equivalents can you get per second of real-world sensory data? Per dollar of capital expenditure?</span></li><li value="3"><span>Ramping up experiments seems especially necessary for medicine and </span><i><span>in vivo </span></i><span>biology, where there are so many complex interactions to predict.</span></li><li value="4"><span>3D printing strikes me as one area where it’s pretty fast to build things, the combinatorial design space is large, and so AI might be able to find a bunch of valuable stuff.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li><li value="3"><b><span>Huge amounts of data exist, and progress so far has been significantly bottlenecked on acquiring or processing this data.</span></b><span> Some guesses: protein structure, genomics, finance.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>Note that data might </span><i><span>become </span></i><span>a bottleneck once AI training eats up the current stock of data. Or once we’re trying to do stuff out of distribution, e.g. designing novel proteins (that look very different from natural ones) as an early step towards advanced nanotechnology.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h4><span>There are also stories for how advanced AIs could route around data bottlenecks:</span></h4><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Advanced AIs will be able to write much better simulations </span></b><span>than currently exist. (Unclear which domains this is true of; seems like a very important question).</span></li><li value="2"><b><span>With existing data, advanced AIs will be able to intuit key principles </span></b><span>that give them a much better ability to “one shot” the problem.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Might be true for some areas of chemistry, molecular biology, &amp; materials science? We have a lot of data and know the underlying principles, and human intuitions for molecular scale behavior are poor, so there might be a bunch of gains to grab here.</span></li><li value="2"><span>I expect this effect is most likely to have impact via catalyzing further improvements: enabling AIs to build better simulations or encouraging companies to invest in real-world deployments or experiments.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h3><span>Which concrete domains see progress?</span></h3><ul><li value="1"><span>LLMs will get very good at coding and math, but in a way that doesn’t generalize to other domains; e.g. their time horizon on these tasks, or their capacity for superhuman performance, will outpace ~all other tasks.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Large neural networks will get very good at other data-heavy areas, once the data pipelines are set up:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Business operations</span></li><li value="2"><span>Robotic operation</span></li><li value="3"><span>Maybe some data-rich areas of science</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>The resulting mixing-and-matching of technical skill, broad domain knowledge, and medium-generality intuitions could dominate existing human organizations in valuable areas.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>E.g. lots of business tasks, smaller-scale coding projects, lots of jobs that mostly need decent qualitative reasoning &amp; attention to detail.</span></li><li value="2"><span>I wouldn’t be surprised if finance got moderately more efficient, despite being a pretty heavily optimized field, because you can do some really sophisticated processing of qualitative data for patterns. (e.g.- auto ingest all company filings, all news stories, in a sophisticated way; parse historical such data for tradeable patterns.)</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>And maybe because context-aware trading systems can e.g. identify anomalies and prevent bad trades by the rules-based systems that do most trading.</span></li></ul></li></ul></li></ul><h2><span>Implications for AI takeoff</span></h2><h3><span>While it lasts, weak fluid intelligence is great news for alignment risk</span></h3><p><b><span>Successful scheming might rely on very good general reasoning. </span></b><span>Otherwise, you could do some combo of…</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Test the AI — regular capability evaluations, use interpretability to try to ID deception, etc.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Run domain-specialized Ais as control-style overseers</span></li><li value="3"><span>Harden the AI’s environment — cybersec measures</span></li><li value="4"><span>Without the ability to run subtle deceptions around multiple layers of oversight, the AI might be cooked.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>It seems really hard to decide to sandbag without this showing up in some layer of oversight, unless you can do complex sandbagging reasoning in a single forward pass.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h3><span>A key bifurcation point: can AIs revolutionize AI R&amp;D, or merely speed it up?</span></h3><p><span>Coding is a data-rich domain, and AI companies prioritize generating data on AI R&amp;D tasks, so we should expect AIs to get better at AI R&amp;D over time — as we indeed see.</span></p><p><span>Case 1: AIs can significantly improve coder productivity and codebase efficiency, but they don’t reach </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/six-milestones-for-ai-automation"><span>supremacy</span></a><span>. R&amp;D progress is gated on compute-hungry research experiments and on expert research taste.</span></p><p><span>Case 2: AIs are able to intuit key principles of AI R&amp;D. This lets them move smoothly from usefulness to outright </span><a href="https://www.planned-obsolescence.org/p/six-milestones-for-ai-automation"><span>supremacy</span></a><span>. The best human experts are great at this, but they’re held back by brains with limited working memory and poor native resources for understanding massive-dimensional spaces and inhuman minds.</span></p><p><b><span>In both cases, my best guess is that improved AI R&amp;D eventually leads to a paradigm that can scale to superhuman fluid intelligence.</span></b><span> And since resources and R&amp;D productivity are scaling so rapidly, “eventually” will probably come pretty soon.</span></p><p><span>But in case 1 especially, we’re likely to see a period where AI architectures evolves a lot. That has important implications:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>AI safety insights might become less transferable across labs</span></li><li value="2"><span>AI safety techniques may no longer work. The fact that </span><a href="https://agarriga.substack.com/i/179897092/honesty-and-goodness-in-models"><span>current LLMs seem pretty aligned</span></a><span> shouldn’t give us much comfort if the final architectures look different.</span></li><li value="3"><span>New, more efficient paradigms could face a dramatic data or </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/computing-overhang"><span>compute overhang</span></a><span>, leading to a sharp jump in capabilities</span></li><li value="4"><span>Frontier AI might be much more vulnerable to theft, because a lot of value is contained in </span><a href="https://situational-awareness.ai/lock-down-the-labs/#Algorithmic_secrets"><span>algorithmic secrets</span></a><span> rather than full model weights or training environments.</span></li></ul><h2><span>Is this the world we live in?</span></h2><p><b><span>Some evidence against: It seems like the “water level” of LLM capabilities is gradually rising in many areas, and that some of this is probably a generalizable-skills thing.</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>E.g.: probably the amount of chess games in base training data didn’t vary a ton between models, at least since 2023. But chess performance has improved a lot, especially with the advent of reasoning models.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>(The two main benchmarks seem to be </span><a href="https://maxim-saplin.github.io/llm_chess/"><span>Saplin</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://dubesor.de/benchtable"><span>Dubesor</span></a><span>. They have different results, and both result sets are a bit weird to someone used to Number Going Up with each model, but they do broadly show a significant leap in performance between the GPT-3 / Claude-2 generation and later models.)</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span>Reasoning models &amp; the ability to improve performance via longer inference suggest some “fluid intelligence” is going on</span></b><span> — enough that you’re not just computing the answer based on simple heuristics and plateauing after a few tokens. (cf </span><a href="https://strangecities.substack.com/p/knowledge-reasoning-and-superintelligence"><span>Owen</span></a><span>)</span></li><li value="3"><span>This is part of why I think it’s useful to have a notion of a sliding scale of fluidity — it’s clear that they’re </span><i><span>not </span></i><span>just parrots, but also that they’re getting a lot out of domain-specific data that they wouldn’t easily be able to reconstruct via pure reasoning.</span></li></ul><h3><span>How can we test this hypothesis?</span></h3><p><b><span>Places to look for fluid reasoning capabilities in LLMs:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span>Recognizing when they’re wrong or uncertain.</span></b></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>LLMs are pretty bad at this right now, I think.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Maybe the form of assistant training is partly to blame? If you only get scored based on immediate responses, “can you tell me more?” or “please clarify” isn’t useful. And I dunno how discerning the human evaluators are — maybe they just reward overconfident slop and insight porn.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><b><span>Self-management </span></b><span>— e.g. Claude Plays Pokemon giving itself bad notes and getting stuck.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span>Meta reasoning</span></b><span>, e.g. identifying “this situation seems contrived”.</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>They do some of this now, e.g. </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23836"><span>noticing they’re likely being evaluated</span></a><span>.</span></li></ul></li><li value="4"><b><span>Performing well when their heuristics need to be reversed. </span></b><span>You could design a “trap” game that preys on people who are using normal heuristics (e.g. a chess variant designed so that controlling the center of the board is bad.)</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="5"><ul><li value="1"><span>A simple example: We’re only recently seeing good performance on reversed riddles — LLMs </span><a href="https://www.oneusefulthing.org/p/on-jagged-agi-o3-gemini-25-and-everything"><span>used to</span></a><span> instead give the answer to the typical riddle, and </span><a href="https://github.com/Syksy/LLMSurgeonSonRiddle"><span>now they notice</span></a><span> and give the right answer.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Counterpoint: humans are often bad at “traps”, and require time to learn new situations. So I’m not sure what a fair comparison point is.</span></li></ul></li><li value="5"><b><span>Performing well on tasks that seem heavily general-reasoning loaded, and definitely weren’t in the training data.</span></b></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="6"><ul><li value="1"><span>Evaluable:</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>A novel game (could compare to dedicated RL systems on the same game).</span></li><li value="2"><span>Making money at scale in the real world.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Are there variants that are more clearly reasoning-loaded?</span></li></ul></li><li value="6"><b><span>Re-learning. </span></b><span>If you unlearn some data or principles from a model, can it rederive that from first principles?</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="7"><ul><li value="1"><span>This question is also relevant for the usefulness of unlearning as a safeguard against AI misuse or misbehavior.</span></li></ul></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><i><span>Thanks to K, Adria, John, and Abi for comments.</span></i></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnamv5g49spqv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefamv5g49spqv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>They’re more like a horde of precocious 12-year olds, each with a different hyperfixation.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Zxw3ZcmSdndpQyJ6M/what-if-llms-are-mostly-crystallized-intelligence#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Your rights when flying to Europe](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/F5KkZiGMytDJszzyg/your-rights-when-flying-to-europe)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>Europe (and the UK) have strong protections for flyers in the case of delayed or cancelled flights. However very few people are aware of these, and airlines will almost always try to wriggle out of paying up.</span></p><p><span>Even travel agents are often unaware of these laws, or unwilling to fight the airline for you.</span></p><p><span>Given the rollercoaster that flying to/from Israel has been in the last 3 years, I've had my share of experience forcing airlines to pay up what they owe, so I thought it might be valuable turning that into a post.</span></p><p><span>These regulations are enshrined in EU 261. You can see the full text </span><a href="https://www.legislation.gov.uk/eur/2004/261/contents" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>, and equally importantly the interpretive guidelines </span><a href="https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/PDF/?uri=CELEX:52016XC0615%2801%29&amp;from=EN" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span> that cover many edge cases.</span></p><h1><span>TLDR</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span>When flying into or out of the EU or UK, consider booking a flight with an EU or UK based airline.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Don't book a car/hotel with the airline, as that turns it into a package deal which has weaker rights.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Preserve records of all interactions with the airline. Prefer text based chat to phone as this is easier to records. If you do phone, and get a negative answer, follow up with a text based chat to preserve a record.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Airlines are required to find you an alternative flight </span><b><span>Under Comparable Conditions </span></b><span> if they cancel your flight. If they don't, you can book one yourself and claim a refund.</span></li><li value="5"><span>If you book an alternative flight, keep all receipts and boarding passes to prove you actually took the flight.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Then ask the airline for a refund, quoting EU 261.</span></li><li value="7"><span>If they refuse, or offer a partial refund, go to arbitration. You will win.</span></li><li value="8"><span>Airlines are required to pay you compensation if they cancel or delay your flight at short notice (2 weeks) without sufficient justification. If they refuse to pay up, go to arbitration. </span></li></ul><h1><span>Which flights do these laws apply to?</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span>Any flights departing an EU, EEA, or UK airport, regardless of airline.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Flights with an EU, EEA, or UK based airline arriving into an EU, EEA, or UK airport.</span></li></ul><p><span>So they apply to a Delta flight from Paris to New York, and to a Lufthansa flight from Dubai to London, but not a Delta flight from New York to Paris.</span></p><h1><span>What are my rights?</span></h1><p><span>You actually have quite a lot - go read </span><a href="https://www.legislation.gov.uk/eur/2004/261/contents" rel="noreferrer"><span>EU 261</span></a><span> if you're interested. However I will focus on three main rights, because these are the most important ones:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Right to compensation, in case of delay, cancellation, or denied boarding. The exact details are finicky, so if that happens to you, go read the relevant laws and see if they apply (or just ask your favourite LLM). But in many cases, you may be owed between 250 - 600 Euro per passenger, depending on the length of the flight.</span></li><li value="2"><span>In case of cancellation, </span><a href="https://www.legislation.gov.uk/eur/2004/261/article/8" rel="noreferrer"><span>alternative rerouting to the final destination under comparable conditions</span></a><span>. Comparable conditions means the same class, and similar length flights/number of stops. If you booked a non-stop flight, they can't force you to accept one with a layover.</span></li><li value="3"><a href="https://www.legislation.gov.uk/eur/2004/261/article/9" rel="noreferrer"><span>Right of care</span></a><span>, including meals, accommodation, and transport, when the cancellation/delay is significant enough to warrant it.</span></li></ol><p><span>In my experience airlines are fairly good about the right of care, so/so about compensation, and absolutely atrocious on rerouting.</span></p><h1><span>How will airlines try to screw you over?</span></h1><p><span>When airlines cancel a flight, they will offer you a refund, or to book an alternative flight with them or their partners. If they cancelled all flights to your destination, and only other carriers still fly, then they will simply shrug and offer you a refund.</span></p><p><span>If you mention EU-261, they will likely claim that it doesn't apply because they informed you about the cancellation more than 2 weeks ahead. This is an attempt to muddy the waters because that only applies to compensation, and you're requesting rerouting.</span></p><p><span>If you push they may say that you can book flights yourself, but only up to a maximum of X dollars per person. This is nonsense, there is no such provision in the law (with the exception where the flights are so expensive they threaten the operation of the airline, this is rarely applicable to large airlines).</span></p><p><span>If you booked via an online travel agent, they may tell you to talk to the travel agent. This is nonsense, the responsibility lies solely on them.</span></p><p><span>When claiming expenses from the airline, they will likely stonewall you, or deny your request.</span></p><p><span>When you get to arbitration they may make up spurious reasons it doesn't apply, or attempt to demand lots of proof from you, in the hope you give up the process.</span></p><h1><span>Your playbook for flight cancellation</span></h1><p><span>As soon as the airline cancels your flight, check if they offer you alternative flights in a reasonable timeframe (I don't know the exact limit, but not more than a few days). If so book them.</span></p><p><span>If they don't, talk to customer support, and ask them to book you an alternative flight. If you are worried that all alternative flights will disappear consider booking alternative flights immediately if your local law allows you to cancel them shortly after booking (Israel gives 48 hours).  I have successfully received compensation for flights booked before the airline had yet refused my request.</span></p><p><span>Whenever you talk to customer support maintain records. The best way to do that is by talking via their chat based interface. If that doesn't take you to a human, repeatedly ask for "speak to agent" until it does. You can usually download the final transcript, but if not you can try saving as PDF, scraping the HTML, or ask an LLM for some script to extract the text via the browser console.</span></p><p><span>Ask for the customer agent to offer you rerouting in accordance to EU 261, and firmly but politely turn down any offers for refunds, flights under worse conditions, offers of flights in 3 months time, etc. Eventually say that as they refuse to abide by EU 261, you will book flights yourself.</span></p><p><span>Look for fairly priced flights under the same conditions, from/to the same source and destination. Don't try and be clever here and buy perks you didn't have on the original ticket. You don't necessarily need to find the cheapest possible tickets if they are at an inconvenient time, but the law isn't clear on this so do so at your own risk. Look for flights as close to the time of your original flight as possible, but if none are available then, you can go further out.</span></p><p><span>Keep your receipts.</span></p><p><span>Keep your boarding passes after the flights (for all passengers).</span></p><p><span>Once the flight has completed, ask the airline to pay for your alternative flights, using their claims portal. If they refuse, offer you partial payment, or do not reply within about 2 months, go to arbitration.</span></p><p><span>Most European airlines are a member of an arbitration scheme. For example British Airways is a member of  </span><a href="https://www.aviationadr.org.uk/"><span>https://www.aviationadr.org.uk/</span></a><span>. Delta is apparently also a member when flying from the UK (but not Europe). This is a good reason to prefer European airlines even when flying from Europe, so consider checking before you fly. I don't have any experience dealing with an airline that wasn't part of an arbitration scheme.</span></p><p><span>The arbitration schemes are all rather similar (awful websites, slow to respond, get you your money eventually), so ask an LLM which one to use for your airline, open a case, do whatever they tell you too, and eventually get your money back.</span></p><h1><span>Your playbook for compensation</span></h1><p><span>If your looking for compensation for a flight cancelled or delayed at short notice, you can skip straight to filing a claim, and going to arbitration. Check first to see whether the law actually applies (you can ask an LLM to help).</span></p><h1><span>Do Nots</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span>Do not book a package holiday, as your rights are far weaker (including no right to re-routing)</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>A package holiday is defined as a combination of at least two different types of travel services (such as transport, accommodation, or car rental) for the same trip, so don't even book a car or hotel with an airline, book them separately instead. </span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Do not lose your evidence</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Make sure you have all communications with the airline in writing, keep receipts, boarding passes, etc.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Do not give in to the airline</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>They will likely try to confuse you or offer you worse offers. Know your rights, and stick to them.</span></li></ul></li></ul><h1><span>What about...?</span></h1><p><span>If you're unsure whether or how the law applies to your case, read the </span><a href="https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/PDF/?uri=CELEX:52016XC0615%2801%29&amp;from=EN" rel="noreferrer"><span>interpretive guidelines</span></a><span>. They are pretty readable and well written.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/F5KkZiGMytDJszzyg/your-rights-when-flying-to-europe#comments">Discuss</a>
